ELECTION COMMISSION , INDIA 

New Delhi, the 3rd January 1968 
S . O . 222 .- In exercise of the powers conferred by sub - section ( 1 ) of section 
13A of the Representation of the People Act , 1950 ( 43 of 1950 ) , the Election Com 
mission , in consultation with the Government of Orissa hereby nominates Shri 
G . N , Das , L. A . S . Additional Secretary to Government Political and Services De 
partment, as the Chief Electoral Officer for the State of Orissa with effect from 
the 15th November , 1967 ( A . N .) and until further orders vice Shri Amar Singh , 
JAS. retired . 

(No. 154 / 10 /67.1 


ORDERS 


Neno Delhi, tie 12th December 1987 


S . 0 . 223 . - Whereas Shri Baserising Kushran a contesting candidate for election 
to the House of the People from the Mandla Constituency, failed to lodge any 
account of his election expenses as required by the Representation of the People 
Act , 1951, and the Rules macle thereunder . and was given due notice of such 
failure : 

And , whereas the Election Comniission is satisfied that the account since lodger! 
by him has also not been lodged in the manner prescribed in the said Art anci 
Rules , and he has not furnished any rood reason or justification for the railure , 

Now , therefore , in pursuance ot Section 10 - A of the said Act, the Election 
Commission hereby declares the sair Shri Bascrising Kushram to be disqualifed 
for being chosen as, and for being, amemher of either House of Parliament or 
of the Legislative Assembly or Legislative Council of 7 State for a period of 
three years from the date of this Order .

New Delhi, the 22117 December 1967 
S . O . 224 .-_ Whereas the Election Commission is satisfied that Shri Menezes 
Armando a contesting candidate for election to the House of the People from 
Marmagoa constituency , has failed to lodge an account of his election expenses 
in the manner required by the Representation of the People Act, 1951, and the 
Rules made thereunder ; 

And whereas , the said candidate even after due notice has not given any good 
reason or explanation for the failure ; 

Now , therefore, in pursuance of section 10A of the said Act, the Election Com 
mission hereby declares the said Shri Menezes Armando to be disqualified for 
being chosen as , and for being , a member of either House of Parliament or of the 
Legislative Assembly or Legislative Council of a State for a period of three years 
from the date of this Order . 

[No, Goa -HP / 2 /67 ( 2 ). 1 


New Delhi, the 1st January 1968 
S . O . 225 . — Whereas the Election Commission is satisfied that Shri Gurdial Singh 
S / o Shri Chana Singh , village Bhaini Bhaga, Tehsil Mansa ( Punjab ) , a con 
testing candidate for election to the House of the People from Bhatinda Consti 
tuency , has failed to lodge an account of his election expenses within the time 
and in the manner required by the Representation of the People Act , 1951, and 
the Rules made thereunder ; 

And whereas, after considering the representation made by the said candidate , 
the Election Commission is further satisfied that he has no good reason or justifl 
cation for the failure ; 

Now , therefore , in pursuance of section 10A of the said Act , the Election Com 
mission hereby declares the said Shri Gurdial Singh to be disqualified for being 
chosen as , and for being, a member of either House of Parliament or of the Legis 
lative Assembly or Legislative Council of a State for a period of three years from 
the date of this Order , 

[No. PB -HP / 13 /67 (2 ). ]

$ . 0 . 226 . - - Whereas the Election Commission is satisfied that Shri Pradhan 
Kishu , village Janoa , P . O . Godda, Santhal Parganas a contesting candidate for 
election to the House of the People from Godda constituency , has failed to lodge 
an account of his election expenses within the time and in the manner required 
by the Representation of the People Act, 1951, and the Rules made thereunder ; 

And whereas , after considering the representation made by the said candidate , 
the Election Commission is further satisfied that he has no good rcason or justi 
fication for the failure ; 

Now , therefore, in pursuance of section 10A of the said Act, the Election Com 
mission hereby declares the said Shri Pradhan Kishu to be disqualified for being 
chosen as , and for being, a member of either House of Parliament or of the 
Legislative Assembly or Legislative Council of a State for a period of three years 
from the date of this order

( 1 ) of section 9 of the Indian Stamp Act , 1899 ( 2 of 1899 ) , the Central Govern 
ment hereby remits (in addition to the remission of duty made in notifcation 
No 13 / 87 of the Government of India in the Ministry of Finance (Department of 
Revenue and Insurance ) and published in the Gazette of India , Part II, section 
3 , sub - section ( ii ) dated 23rd December, 1967 at page 4747) , the stamp duty with 
which the ad -hoc bonds of the value of tour lakhs of rupees to be issued by the 
Jammu and Kashmir State Financial Corporation , are chargeable under the said 
Act 

(No. 1 / 68 -F . No. 1 / 72 / 67 -Cus .VII / Stamps. ]

8 . 0 230 . - - In exercise of the powers conterred by clause ( a ) of sub - section ( 1 )

CENTRAL BOARD OF DIRECT TAXES 

INCOMETAX 

New Delhi, the 4th January 1968 
8 . 0 . 23L - In exercise of the powers conferred by Sub - section ( 1 ) of Section 
122 of the Income- tax Act , 1961 (43 of 1961 ) and of all other power , enabling 
it in that behalf , the Central Board of Direct Taxes, hereby makes the following 
further amendments in the Schedule appended to its notification No. 08 ( F . No. 
50 / 88 / 07 -ITJ) dated 9th August, 1967 namely : 
Against Poona Range - II Poona under column 2 the following shall be added : 
16 Additional Income- tax Officer , F -Ward , Poona. 

Explanatory Note 
The amendment has become necessary on account of the creation of a new 
charge in the Commissioner s charge . 

( This note does not form part of the notification but is intended to be merely 
clarifcatory .) 

[No. I/ F . No. 50 / 88 / 87-ITJ .] 
New Delhi, the 8th January 1988 
9 . 0 232 . - In exercise of the powers conferred by sub - section ( 1 ) of section 
122 of the Income- tax Act, 1961 (43 of 1961) , and of all other powers enabling 
it in that behalf , and in supersession of all the previous notifications in this regard , 
the Central Board of Direct Taxes hereby directs that the Appellate Assistant 
Commissioners of Income-tax of the Ranges specified in column 2 of the Schedule 
below shall perform their functions in respect of all persons and incomes assessed 
to Encome-tax or Super- tax in the Income-tax Circles , Wards or District specified

Where an Income-tax Circle , Ward or District or part thereof stands transferred 
by this Notification from one Range to another Range , appeals arising out of 

spessments made in that Income- tax Circle , Ward or District or part thereof 
and pending Immediately before the date of this Notification before the Appellato 
Avuistani Commissioner of the range from whom that Income-tax Clrcle , Ward 
or District or part thereof is transferred shall, from the date of this Notification 
shall take eflect, be transferred and dealt with by the Appellate Assistant Com 
missioner of the Range to whom the said Circle , Ward or District or part thereof 
is tracistcrred . 
This Notification shall take effect from the 15th January , 1968. 

Explanatory Note 
The amendments have become necessary on account of the abolition of C 
Range Patna and Jamshedpur Range , Jamshedpur and creation of B Range , 
Cuttack in the Commissioner s charge and consequent revision of jurisdiction 
amongst the AACS.

New Delhi, the 6th January 1968 
S . O . 249 , - In exercise of the powers conferred by section 3 of the Agricul 
tural Produce (Grading and Marking ) Act, 1937 ( 1 of 1937 ) , the Central Govern 
ment hereby makes the following rules , the same having been previously publish 
ed namely - 

1 Short title and application . - ( 1) These rules may be called the Fennel 
(Whole and Ground ) , Fenugreck (Whole and Ground ) and Celery Seeds (Whole ) 
Grading and Marking Rules, 1967 . 

( 2 ) They shall apply to the Fennel (Whole and Ground ) ( Foeniculum Vul 
gare Mill ) , Fenugreek (Whole and Ground ) ( Trigonella foenumgraecum L . ) and 
Celery Seed (Whole ) ( Apium graveolens L . ) grown in India , 
2 Definitions. In these rules -- 
( 1 ) " Agricultural Marketing Adviser " means the Agricultural Marketing 

Adviser to the Government of India 
(2 ) " Schedule" means a Schedule appended to these rules 
3 . Grade designations. Grade designations to indicate the quality of the seed 
shall be as set out in column 1 of Schedules 11 to VI. 

4 . Definition of quality . The quality indicated by the respective grade desig 
nations and the general characteristics shall be as set out against cach grade 
designation in columns 2 to 7 and 8 respectively of Schedule II, columns 2 to 4 
and 5 respectively of Schedules III and V , columns 2 to 6 and 7 respectively of 
Schedule IV and columns 2 to 4 and 5 respectively of Schedule VI. 

5 Grade designation marks. ( 1 ) The grade designation marks in the case of 
Fennel (Whole and Ground ) or Fenugreek (Whole and Ground ) or Celery Seed 
(Whole ) packed in polythene and / or paper bags shall consist of a design incor 
porating the number of Certi [lcate of Authorisation , the word AGMARK and 
the grade approved by the Agricultural Marketing Adviser . 

( ij ) The grade designation mark in the case of Fennel (Ground ) or Fenugreek 
Ground ) , packed in tins or glass bottles shall consist of a paste - on label, speci 
fying the grade designation and bearing the design consisting of an outlinę map 
of India with the word Agmark 

( iii ) The grade designation mark in the case of Fennel Seeds (Whole and 
Ground ) , or Fenugreek ( Whole and Ground ) or Celery Seeds (Whole ) packed in 
containers of jute or cloth as well in containers in which sealed polythene bags 
of graded Fennel (Whole and Ground ) or Fenugreek (Whole and Ground ) or 
Celery Seeds (Whole ) are packed shall consist of a label, specifying the grade 
designation and bearing the design ( consisting of an outline map of India with 
the word Agmark and the figure of the rising sun , with the Produce of India 
and ( IT 3777 * ) resembling the one as set out in Schedule I. 


6 . Method of marking - ( 1 ) The grade designation mark shall be securely 
aflxed to or prioted on each container in a manner approved by the Agricultural 
Marketing Adviser 

( 2 ) In addition to the above , the following particulars shall also be clearly 
and Indelibly marked on each container 

(1 ) Date of packing in code or plain letters. 
( il) Lot number. 

( ill) Net weight 

( 3 ) An authorised packer may, after obtaining the prlor approval of the Agri 
cultural Marketing Adviser , mark his private trade mark on a container , in a 
manner approved by the said oficer provided that the private trade mark does 
not represent a quality or grade different from what is indicated by the grade 
designation mark affixed to the container in accordance with these rules . 

7 Method of picking ( 1 ) Only sound , clean and dry containers made of jute, 
cloth , paper or polythene shall be used for packing. They shall be free from any 
Insect infestation or fungus contamination and also free from any undesirable 
sinell. 

( 2 ) The containers shall be securely closed and sealed in a manner approved 
by tbe Agricultural Marketing Adviser. 


dir Or Tutor : JA VAKY 20 , 1908 / TAUSA 20, 1889 


PART II 


( 3 ) Each package shall contain Fennel (Whole and Ground ) or Fenugreek 
(Whole and Ground ) or Celery Seeds (Whole) of one grade designation only . 

8 . Special conditions of Certificate of Authorisation . In addition to the condi 
tions specified in rule 4 of the General Grading and Marking Rules, 1937 , the 
following special conditions shall be observed by packers to the satisfaction of 
the Agricultural Marketing Adviser , 

(i ) an authorised packer shall make such arrangements for testing Fennel 
(Whole and Ground ) 0 : Fenugreek (Whole and Ground ) or Celery (Whole ) , 28 
may be prescribed from time to time by the Agricultural Marketing Adviser. 

( 11 ) An authorised packer shall provide all facilities , as may be necessary to 
the Inspecting Officer , duly authorised by the Agricultural Marketing Adviser in 
this behalf.

granted by the University of Oklahoma, United State of America for the purposes 
of the said Act ;

with the law regulating the registration of medical practitioners in his country

ferred to this Tribunal by Notification No 8 / 25 / 67 -LRII dated 25th April, 1967 
In initial stages of the hearing of this case an objection was raised by the manage 
ment, the Indian Copper Corporation Ltd , that the complainant was not a coll 
( ernod workman in the parent case After this question was decided by means of 
a comprehensive order covering all the cases between the parties and issues had 
been settled , a date for hearing was fixed in the case. Before, however, evidence 
could be recorded , the Union , Mosaboni Mines Labour Union , which represented the 
complainant entered into a package deal and compromised most of the disputes with 
the management including the dispute in this case. The terms of settlement are 
Annexure " A " to this order Before , however, it could be accepted and final order 
passed the management attempted to resile from the compromise settlement and 
after hearing arguments on the legal aspect of the matter an order was recorded 
on 20 / 28th September , 1967 which is Annexure " B " to this order The management, 
however , later on withdrew their application and intimated by an application dated 
28th September 1967 that they would abide by the compromise yettlement Para 2 
of the compromise settlement would show that the complainant, Şri Bhattacharjee , 
will be given a special increment of 0 ,42 p . with effect froin 1st August 1967, an 
oller which has been accepted by the Union on behalf of the complainant The 
controversy is therefore , satisfactorily resolved and an order is recorded in terms 
thereof Since, however , the order amounts to an award let the same be submitted 
to Government of India , Ministry of labour & Employment, for publication in the

would also stand transferrent to this Tribunal. In conscquence of the aforesaid 
order two References Nos. 4 and 79 /85 of industrial disputes between the Indian 
Copper Corporation Ltd . (to be hereinafter described as Corporation ) and their 
workmen were transferred to this Tribunal. In connection with these references 
10 cases under Section 33- A filed by individual workmen against the Corporation 
and 137 cases under Section 33 ( 2 ) ( b ) or 33 ( 3 ) ( h ) for approval for permission 
(lled by the Corporation were also transferred to this Tribunal. A list of these 
rases are appended with this Order as Annexure " A " A few applications were

tion in respect of action taken against certain other workmen and these are also 
stateil in the Annexure " A " . There is only one Union operating , both for the 
inincs and is named as Mosaboni Mines Labour Union , to be hereinafter called , 
the Union . In all these cases the Corporation desired this Tribunal to determine 
the question whether the workman in every casc was a concerned workman or 
not, 29 À preliminary question on the basis of observations maile hy the Hon ble

of facts and observations the legal position may now be examined . 


2. The Industrial Disputes Act defines a settlement in Scction 2 ( p ) as follows: 
" Setlenenl" means a settlement arrived at in the course of conciliation 

proceedings and includes a written agreement hetween the emplo ver 
and workmen arriyed at otherwise than in the course of concillation 
proceeding where such agreement has been signed hy the parties 
thereto in such manner as may he prescribe and a copy thereof 


50 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : JANUARY 20 , 1968 / PAUSA 80 , 1889 


[ PART II 


has been sent to the appropriate Government and the Concliation 

Officer " , 
It defines a settlement in two categories , namely , those which are arrived at in 
the course of conciliation proceedings and such which are arrived at otherwise 
than in the course of concliiation proceedings For the second ( ategory , there 
are certain requirements which have been prescribed under Rule 58 ol Industrial 
Disputes (Central) Rules, besides the requirementy that a copy of the same has 
to be sent to appropriate Government and the Conciliation Officer. Such i 
settlement ponivea otherwiec than in the course of conciliatior proceedings 
under Section 18 ( 1 ) It is binding on the parties to the agreenient only The 
Arst category of settlement arrived at in the course of conciliation pro eedings 
ay also arbitration award or an award of Labour Court of Tribunal unrier Sub 
section ( 3 ) O ; Section 18 they are binding on all parties to the industrial dispute 
including the entire body of workmen as stated in Clause 3 ( d ) . The Act makes 
no provision about the position of a settlement arrived at otherwise than In 
onciliation proceedings but during the pendency of an Industrial dispute betore 
a Labour Court or Tribunal. When a reference is pending before a Labour ( ourt 
or Tribunal whether a compromise petition fulfils the requirements of the definition 
of Section 2 ( p ) I. D . Act or not , if the same is a reasonable and just settlement 
and is accepted by the Labour Court or Tribunal, it is incorporated in the award 
and then Section 18 ( 3 ) is attracted . This power of the Labour Court or Tribunal 
to accept the compromise in the award is no more in doubt. In Poona Mazdoor 
Sabha V , Dhutta and another reported in 1956 - II - LLJ p . 319 at page 321 Chagla 
C . J. made some doubtful observation as follows : 
" Industrial law takes no notice of any private settlement or agreement 

arrived at between the parties in the course of an industrial dispule 
Such a private agreement belongs to the rcalm of contract : it may 
give rise to contractual rights ; but when we are dealing with ill 
dustrial law it has no sanction whatsoever ." 


But another portion of the judgement at pagc 323 however , nullified the sting of 
of the above observation . It runs as follows: 
" Industrial pence demands that sanctity should be attached to agreements 

freely arrived at by the parties and if the view went abroad lhot 
private settlements have no sanctity whatsoever , then there would 
be little chance of disputes ending by settlement between the parties. 
It will indeed be extremely unfortunate from the point of view of 

labour " . 
From the aforesaid subsequent observation of the Learned Chief Justice , it is 
clear that sanctity was attached to agreements freely arrived at and should be 
honoured and acted upon by the Tribunals . The Kerala High Court in 1957 
( II ) LLJ p . 45 Krishnakutty Nair V . Industrial Tribunal considered this question 
as also the rights of the parties to adjust their differences wholly or in part by 
lawful agreements or compromise The following is a material passage which 
needs reproduction : 
" Now , it is the elementary right of the parties to a dispute to adjust their 

matters of difference wholly or in part , by any lawful agreement or 
compromise. And every authority entrusted with the task of adjudi 
cating , the dispute was a duty , not a discretion , to record such agree 
ment or compromise subject possibly to an inherent power or retusal 
when substantial injustice would be worked . A Tribunal therelore, 
to whom an industrial dispute is referred for adjudication under 
Section 10 ( 1 ) ( C ) of the Industrial Disputes Act, must record a lawful 
agreement or compromise placed before it by the disputants There 
is nothing in the Act to suggest that a particular class of agreement 
or compromise is outside its scope , though whether a particular 
compromise is lawful or not will very with the varying character 
of dilleront disputes The expression " determination " in the defini 
tion of " award " in the Act indicates only a coming to an end , may 
be in any way whatever , though it may require examination and 

choice " , 
On the principle enunciated in the above ruling , the Inxustrial Tribunals have 
been accepting compromise agreements arrived at freely and lawfully and incor 
porated the same in their awards. The question , however , is whether a party 
having entered into a lawful agreement has an unfettered and inherent right 
without showing just cause to resile from the same. There is no direct case 
law on the point but a few which have some relevancy may be stated 


ated womments arrived at freely and lawfuy 


Sec. 3( ii)]

251 


3 . The Madras High Court in workers employed In 32 textiles mills , Coimbatore 
District Mill Workers Union Vs. Dhanalakshmi Mills Ltd . 1960 ( II) LLJ p . 550 
A . I. R . 1960 (Mad ) page 212 observed that the Industrial Tribunals have no power 
similar to one under Order XXIII R3 CPC to record a compromise. In the 
next paragraph , however , the position was made clear by the following passage : 
" this does not, however, mean that the Tribunal is precluded from taking 

note of a compromise entered into between the workers and the 
management where there is a compromise , it should consider 
whether , in its opinion , the cornpromise could be adopted as its own 
determination of the dispute , l. e . whether it is fair , just and equitable 

between the parties " . 
4 In the ruling under consideration , three of the Union and the management 
of the mills entered into a compromise agrcement. Another Union which was 
not a party to the agreement but represented a substantial number of workmen 
of the mills did not accept the compromise and desired determination by the Tri 
bunal. The Tribunal overruled the objection and aacepting the compromise 
agreement recorded an award . It was in this context that the Hon ble High Court 
made the above observation and held that as the Union was entitled to be heard 
under Section 36 I. D Act, the Tribunal was wrong in ignoring the request and 
accepting the compromise.

5 . The Allahabad High Court in a case Raza Textile Labour Union Vs. R . Mohan 
reported in I. F .LR . 1964 (8 ) p . 308 which has some bearing on the question con 
sidered the effect of a settlement before a statutory arbitrator under U . P . Industrial 
Disputes Act and which was not registered as required by Section 6B of the 
U . P . Act This Section requires an agreement to be registered before the Concili 
ation Omcer in the prescribed manner if arrived at outside concillation proceedings . 
It was held that Section 6B was a self contained provision and the unregistered 
settlement Incorporated in the award was binding by virtue of Section 6 ( 5 ) of 
the Act . The case is therefore, is of no material help except for the position 
that a settlement not having been brought about in a prescribed manner can 
nevertheless he acted upon . The material clue , however , is furnished by the 
observation of the Hon ble Supreme Court ini Swadeshi Cotton Mills Vs. Rajeshwar 
Pragad and others reported in 1960 II-LLJ P 707. That was a case where after 
the decision of the Labour Appellate Tribunal, the parties entered into a com 
promise in respect of an appeal pending before the Hon ble Court . The Supreme 
Court framed two issues , one on the factual aspect of the comrpomise and 
second , whether it was a valid one , and remitted them for a Anding from the 
Tribunal. The Tribunal found that the compromise had in fact taken place and 
was a valid one The Supreme Court accepted the findings and disposed of the 
uppeal in terms of the compromise At page 710 , the following material obser 
vation was made - 


" Just as an industrial dispute could have been settled between the parties 

either before it was referred for adjudication to the Industrial 

Tribunal, or after it was referred and before the award was pro 
nounced by the Tiibunal, so would it be open to the parties to 
settle the dispute so long as it was pending either before the Labour 

Appellate Tribunal or before the Court" . 
From the above observation it is clear that even during the pendency of a case 
Defore a Tribunal the parties can legitimately compromise the dispute . In the 
last but one paragraph of the judgment at page 711 , the following observation 
" linches the whole matter - 
The compromise in question is intended to be Aled in this Court for the 

purpose of enabling the parties to request this Court to pass an 
order in terms of the said compromise . The procedure for obtaining 
such an order which was to be follower is the procedure prescribed 
by the rules of this Court just AS, if a compromise was reached 
before the Tribunal, the procedure to he followed before it would 
he the procedure prescribed by its rules " . 


From the above observation , it is evident that when a compromise is filed before 
the tribunal, the tribunal has to ascertain Arstly , the fact of the compromise and 
secondly the validity thereof Even though , therefore , provisions of Order XXIII 
Rule 3 C . P C ., may not be available to the tribunal, yet on general principles 
If a valid compromise has been reached it would be the duty of the tribunal to 
examine the same and accept the same if found valid . After all, a compromise 
iş a contract and under Section 10 of the Indian Contract Act all agreements 


252 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : JANUARY 20, 1968 / PAUSA 30 , 1889 


(PART II 


are contracts if they are made by the free consent of the parties competent to 
contract, for a lawful consideration and with a lawful object and are not hereby 
expressly declared to be void " . Consequently, the rule incorporated in Order 
XXIII R . 3 C . P . C . is based on the sound principle of freedom of contracts and 
the requirements are the same as are found in the definition of Section 10 of 
the Contract Act. Even though , thicrercre , O . XXIII R 3 CP. C . may not be 
applicable yet the tribunal would apply the general principles , particularly when 
the object of industrial adjudication is to achieve Industrial peace and settle the 
disputes on considerations of social justice , When no rules of procedure are 
prescribed regarding any compromise arrived at during the pendency of a case 
before the Tribunal, the general principle will apply on the lines indicated by 
the Hon ble Supreme Court in Swadeshi Cotton Mills case ( supra ) . 

6 . It is, therefore , held that the Corporation has not got an unfettered right 
to resile from the compromise arrived at in these cases and the fact of tho 
compromise to gather with its validity will have to be enquired into as a question 
of fact . The parties will, therefore , be directed to adduce necessary evidence on 
the point and wlll be informed accordingly . 

7. After the order was dictated on 20th September , 1967 and before it could 
be transcribed and signed the representative of the managernent, Shri K . C . Goel, 
has applied that the application dated 30th August, 1967 restling from the com 
promise is not being pressed and the management would abide by the compromise 
settlement arrived at on 4th August , 1967 That being so , there is no neressity 
left to call for evidence on the point of fact whether there was a compromise 
entered into and the same was valid and lawful one

ORDER /AWARD 
These were two complaints Aled by two employees of Indian Copper Corpora 
tion Ltd ., P . O . Mosaboni Mines under Section 33 - A I. D . Act, Complainant in case 
No, 21 /67 is one Sri Periappa Naidu and in case No . 23 / 67 the complainant is 
Sri R . Ramaswamy. It was alleged that during the pendency of References Nos, 4 
and 79 /67 of the Dhanbad Tribunal which were transferred to this Tribunal and 
were numbered as 72 / 67 and 82 / 67 respectively conditions of the service of the 
complainants have been changed inasmuch as they have been denied the bencfit 
of earned leave during the year 1965 and therefore conditions of service have been 
changed . Both these complaints were also transferred along with the parent 
references by Notification No . 8 / 25 /67 -LR - II dated 25th April, 1967. In the initial 
stages the management raised a plen that the complainants were not the concern 
ed workmen in the references in question of industrial dispute . After this ques 
tion was decided by a comprehensive order in respect of these and other similar 
cases, dates for hearing were fixed after settlement of issues. Before , however, 
evidence could be recorded the management and the Union , Mosaboni Mines 
Labour Union , entered into a pack deal compromising most of their cases includ . 
ing these two cases . The management after Aling the compromise petition which 
is annexure " A " to this order first attempted to resile from the compromise settle 
ment and for which after hearing arguments , an order was recorded which is 
Annexure B to this order . The management, however , later on withdrew the 
objection and accepted the compromise agreement by means of application which 
is " C " this order . As the terms of the compromise settlement would show the 
management has agreed to allow earned leave to both the complainants for the 
year 1965 . The controversy is thus resolved to the satisfaction of the com 
plainants and an order is recorded accordingly . A copy of this order be placed 
in the record of each case . 

Since the order amounts to an award it shall be submitted to the Government 
of India , Ministry of Labour and Employment, for publication in the Gazette of 
India .

Whereas in its letters No. MMLU / 103 /MS /64 dated 3rd October, 1984, and

(Badge No. 5998 ) and for supply of free electricity and fuel to Shri U . C . Patnaik 
( Badge No. 1706 ) respectively . 


And whereas these disputes were referred by the Government of India in the 
Ministry of Labour, Employment and Rehabilitation to the Central Government 
Industrial Tribunal for adjudication . 

And whereas during the pendency of the aforesaid references before the Indus 
trial Tribunal, various applications u / s . 33 ( 2 ) (b ) and u / 8 . 33 ( 3 ) ( b ), of the Indus 
trial Disputes Act have been made by the Management and complaints u / s . 33A 
of the Industrial Disputes Act have been made by the certain workmen . 

And whereas the aforesaid two References are pending as No . 72 of 1967 and

Jabalpur and the cases under Sections 33 and 33 A I. D . Act are also pending before 
the game Tribunal and numbered as detailed in the Schedules A and B annexed 
hereto . 

And whereas the Parties hercto , with a view to create better understanding , 
promote healthy Industrial Relations and maintain Industrial peace, after re 
peated discussions have arrived at an amicable settlement on this Friday, the 4th 
of August 1967 on the following terms and conditions:

ORDER 
1. By Notification No 8 / 25 / 67 -LRII , dated April 25 , 1967, the Ministry of Labour. 
Employment & Relabilitation , Government or India , cxercising powers under section 
33B I. D . Act iransferred 100 cases which were reference of Industrial Disputes . 
Under Section 10 I. D . Act pending before the Central Government Industrial Tri 
bunal, Dhanbad lo this Tribunal By the same order , it was stated that all cases 
arising out of those references, eitlier under Section 33 - A I. D , Act would also stand 
transferred to this Tiibunal. In consequence oť the aforcsaid order , two References 
Nos . 4 and 79 / 05 of industrial disputes between the Indian Copper Corporation 
Ltd . ( to be hereinafter described as Corporation ) and their workmen were transfer 
red to this Tribunal. In conneclion with these references 10 cases under Section 
33 - A Aled by individual workman against the Corporation and 137 cases under 
Section 33 ( 2 ) ( b ) or 33 ( 3 ) ( b ) for approval for permission Aled by the Corporation 
were also transferred to this Tribunal. A list of these cases are appended with 
this Order as Annexure " A " . A few applications were also received subsequent to 
the transfer of the main references , from the Corporation in respect of action taken 
against certain other workmen and these are also stated in the Annexure " A " . 
There is only one Union operating , both for the mines and is named as Mosaboni 
Mines Labour Union , to bc hereinafter called , the Union . In all these cases the 
Corporation desired this Tribunal to determine the question whether the workman 
in every case was a concerned workman or rot, as a preliminary question on the 
basis of observations made by the Hon ble Supreme Court in Tata Iron & Steel 
Co. Ltd ., Vs. D . R . Sinch reported in 1965 ( II) LLJ p . 122 . The Union claimed re 
presentation in 57 cases ncntioned in list but they had authority from the workmen 
concerned in some and not in others . The Union , however, undertook to Alle the 
requisite authority in the remaining cases in which thcy have no such authority . 
Full- fledged arguments on the legal aspect of the matter were , therefore , heard so 
as to record a comprehensive order to govern all cases of the Coporation in which 
they wanted determination of this question as a preliminary point This was done 
at camp Allahabad on 14th July 1967. After hearing arguments from both sides 
an order was recorded on 25th July 1967 which se annexure " B " to this order . The 
order disposed of the preliminary objection raised by the Corporation in so far as 
purely the legal aspect of the matter was concerned and was passed for all the 
cases of the Corporation irrespective of the fact whether the workman concerned 
was or was not represented through the Union . After the said order was passed in 
respect of those cases in which Unlon claimed representation , whether being re 
ferences or applications under Section 33 - A or 33 ( 2 ) (h ) or 33 ( 3 ) (b ) dates were 
fixed for evidence and hearing between 29th August and 3rd September , 1967 at 
Camp Ghatsila , rather Mosaboni . Meanwhile , telegram was received on 11th 
August 1967 that most of the caens were being negotiated for compromise . Since , 
however , there were many cases in which Union was not representative of the 
opposite party in rases under Sortion 33 rates for hearing were altcted but the 
tour programme was curtailed for three dates on 29th , 301h and 31st August, 1967. 
On 29th August , 1967 compromise pe ition was Aled by the parties in all the cases 
From the terms of settlement main Reference No. 72 / 67 was compromised and 
some cases under Section 33- A and 33 ( 2 ) ( b ) or 33 ( 3 ) ( b ) , were also compromised . 
Particulars of these cases are mentioned in paragraphs 2 & 3 of the compromise 
petition . In para 4 of the petition it was stated this main Reference No . 82 /67, 
one case under Section 33 - A (Nr 22 / 67 ) alıd seven cases under Section 33 parti. 
cuilars of which were stated in tlie compromise otition remained unsettled and 
were to he decided by the Tribunal. In para 5 of the petition , it was further stated 
that all other applications under Section 33 shall not be opposed or contested before 
the Tribunal and shall be decided in favour of the management The petition was 
signed by the representatives of the managevient and the Union . In furtherance of 
this compromise petition , two cases under Secr10 . 33- No. 15 / B7 ond 24 / 67 were 
disposed of and the terms of compromise for these cases were accented. In other 
words, the compromise petition was acted upon by the parties at least on this 
date . For such cases which were not compromised and were stated in paragraph 
4 It was decided to commenre hearing and in fact main Reference No . 82 / 67 and 
another case unler Section 33 - A No. 22 / 67 were heard and evidence was recorded 
in subsequent dates When the hearing commencerl on 31st August, 1967 at camp 
Mosaboni applications were Alert on behalf of the management in 19 cases that 
the management would not abide by the arttlement for rertain reasons stated in 
the applications, the settlement having been brought about under false representa 
tion and was voidable at the option of the Corporation Compromise petition 14 


266 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : JANUARY 20, 1968 / PAUSA $ 0, 1889 


[PART II 


annexure " C " to this order . The application of the management is annexure " D " . 
After hearing preliminary arguments on this application of the management it 
was decided to hear full- fledged arguments purely on the point of law whether the 
management has an unfettered right to reşile from the compromise settlement and 
6th September , 1967 was Axed for the purpose at camp Dhanbad . It was expressly 
stated that purely the legal aspect of the question should be deleted and not on 
the justifiability or otherwise of the action of the management. The representa 
tives of the parties argued the law point but without rendering any real assistance 
to the Tribunal on the question of law . Some cases were cited by the representa 
tive of the management, most of which were irrelevant None was cited on behalf 
of the Union I have, however , endeavoured to And out any parallel case for 
guidance but I must confess that I could not come across any in my search . It is 
presumably because this is a unique case of its kind wherein the parties having 
Irrived at & pack deal for compromise of cases pending before the Tribunal, one 
of them , rightly or wrongly felt aggrieved and attempted to resile from the position 
before the compromise could be examined and accepted in all the cases so as to be 
incorporated in the awards of this Tribunal and before a finality could be achieved . 
With the above narration of facts and observations, the legal position may now be 
examined . 
2 Tlie Industrial Disputes Act defines a settlement in Section 20p ) as follows: 
" Settlement" means a settlement arrived at in the course of conciliation 

proceedings and includes a written agreement between the employer 
and workmen arrived at otherwise than in the course of conciliation 
proceeding where such agreement has been signed by the parties 
thereto in such manner as may be prescribed and a copy thereof has 
been sent to the appropriate Government and the Conciliation Omcer " . 


It defines & settlement in two categories , namely , those which are arrived at In 
the course of conciliation proceedings and such which are arrived at otherwise 
than in the course of conciliation proceedings . For the second category , there are 
rertain requirements which have been prescribed under Rule 58 of Industrial Dis 
putes (Central) Rules, besides the requirements that a copy of the same has to 
he sent to appropriate Government and the Conciliation omrer . Such & settlement 
arrived at otherwise that in the course of conciliation proceedings under Section 
18 ( 1 ) it is binding on the parties to the agreement only . The first category of 
settlement arrived at in the course of conciliation proceedings as also arbitration 
award or an award of Labour Court of Tribunal under Şub section ( 3 ) of Section 
18 they are binding on all parties to the industrial dispute including the entire 
Dody of workmen as stated in Clause 3 ( a ). The Act makes no provision about 
the position of a settlement arrived at otherwise than in conciliation proceedings 
hut during the pendency of an industrial dispute before a Labour Court or Tribunal. 
When a reference is pending before a Lahour Court or Tribunal whether a com 
promise petition fulAls the requirements of the definition of Section 2 ( p ) I . D . Act 
or not, it the same is a reasonable and just settlement and is accepted by the 
Labour Court or Tribunal, it s incorporated in the award and then Section 18 ( 3 ) 
is attracted . This power of the Labour Court or Trlhunal to accept the com 
promise in the award on award is no more in doubt In Poona Mazdoor Sabha y . 
Dhutta and another reported in 1958 - II-LI J 319 at page 321 Chagla C . J. made 
some doubtful observation as follows: 
" Industrial law takes no notice of any private settlement or agreement 

arrived at between the parties in the course of an industrial dispute . 
Such a private agreement belongs to the realm of contract ; it may 
give rise to contrartual rights; but when we are dealing with industrial 
law it has no sanction whatsoever. " 


But another portion of the judgment at page 323 however , nulined the sting of 
lie alove observation . It runs as follows : — 
" Industrial peace demands that sanctity should be attached to agreements 

freely arrived at by the parties and the view went abroad that 
private settlements have no sanctity whatsoever , then there would 
he little chance of disputes ending by settlement between the parties 
It will indeed be extremely unfortunate from the point of view of 
labour" . 


From the aforesaid subsequent observation of the Learned Chief Justice it is clear 
hat sanctity was attached to agreements freely arrived at and should be honoured 
ind acted upon by the Tribunals The Kerala High Court In 1957 ( II ) LIJ 45 
Krishnakutty Nair V . Industrial Tribunal considered this question as also the 


Séc, anh 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA :, JANUARY 20 , 1968 / PAUSA 30 , 1889 


R67 


rights of the parties to adjust their differences wholly or in part by lawful agte 
ments or compromiso . The following is a material passage which needs reproduc. 
tion 


" Now , it is the elementary right of the parties to a dispute to adjust their 

matters of difference wholly or in part , by any lawful agreement or 
rolnpromise . And every authority entrusted with the task of adjudi 
cating , the dispute was a duty , not a discretion , to record such agree 
ment or compromise gubject possibly to an inherent power of refusal 
when substantial injustice would be worked . A Tribunal therefore , 
to whom an industrial dispute is referred for adjudication under 
Section 10 ( 1 ) ( C ) of the Industrial Disputes Act , inust record a lawful 
agreement or compromise placed before it by the disputants . There 
is nothing in the Act to suggest that a particular class of agreement 
or compromise is outside its scope , though whether a particular com 
promise 18 lawful or not will vary with the varying character of dif 
ferent disputes . The expression " determination " in the definition of 
" Award in the Act indicates only a coming to an end , may be in any 
war whatever , though it may require examinalion and choice " 


On the principlo cnunciated in the above ruling, the Industrial Tribunals have been 
accepting compromise agreements arrived at freely and lawfully and incorporated 
the same in their awards. The question , however . is whether a party having 
antered irito a lawful agreement has an unfettered and inherent right without 

howing just cause to resile from the same. There is no direct case law on the 
Moint but a few which have ronie relevancy may be stated . 


3 . The Madras High Couit in workers employed in 32 textiles mills . Coimbatore 
District Mill Workers Union Vs. Dhanalakshmi Mills Ltd . 1960 ( II) LLJ p . 556 — . 
7 . I. R . 1960 (Mad ) page 212 observed that the Industrial Tribunals have no power 
milar to one under Order XXIII R3 C . P . C . to record a compromise . In the next 
aragraph , however, the position was made clear by the following passage : 


" This does not, however, mean that the Tribunal is precluded from taking 

note of a compromise entered into between the workers and tho 
management. Where there is a compromise , it should consider 
whether , in its opinion the compromise could be adopted as its ow 
determination of the dispute, i. e . whether it is talr , just and equitable 
between the parties” . 


4 . In the ruling under consideration , three of the Unions and the management 
of the mills entered into a compromise agreement. Another Union which was not 
ct party to the agreement but represented a substantial number of workmen of the 
171118 did not accept the rainpromise and desirer determination by the Tribunal. 
The T - ihungl Overruled the cliention and accepting the compromise agreement 
lecorded ati award I was in this context that the Hon ble High Court made the 
above observation and held that as the Union was entitled to be heard under 
Section 36 - 1 . D . Art, the Tribunal was wrong in ignoring the request and accepting 
the compromise 


15 . The Allahabad High Court tu a case Raza Textile Labour Union Vs. R . Mohan 
reported in I. F .L . L . 1964 ( 8 ) p . 306 which has some bearing on the question con 
- idered the effect of a settlement before a statutory Arbiurator under U . P . Industrial 
Disputes Act and which was not registered as required , Section 6B of the U . P 
Act. This Sectico requires an agreement to be registered bc lore the Conciliation 

Acer in the prerrrlei mancr if arrived at outside conciliation proceedings. It 
Las held the Serton 6 - B was a self contained provision and the unregistered settle 

ent incipit lid in the 211ord Tus binding by virtue of Section 6 (5 ) of the Act. 
The case is thereto pot ng mat rial help except for thi po ition that a settle 
inent not ha Ina In Tigh . Olt in i prescribed manner can nevertheless be 
icted upon . The material cluc, however , 14 furnished hy the observation of the 
ilon se Supone jury in Swadeshi Cotton Mills Vs . Rajeshwar Prasad and others 
icportert in 1967 - 11- ! L p . 707. That was :1 ( 25e where after the decision ot the 
labour Appellato Tribunal , the parties ente ed into a comoromise in regrert of an 
ppeal pinding ra the Hon ble Court The Surreme Court er med two Isgres , 
ne on the factual aspect of the compromise and second , whethir It was a valid one 
ud rongitted their for A finding from the Tribunal The Tribunal found that the 


PART 11 


268 


THE GAZE I CE OF INDIA : JANUARY 20 , 1988 / PAUSA 30 , 1889 


compromise had in fact taken place and was a valid one. The Supreme Court ac 
cepted the findings and disposed of the appeal in terms of the compromise At 
page 710 , the following material observation was made : 


" Just as an industrial dispute could have been settled between the parties 

either before it was referred for adjudication to the Industrial Tribunal 
or after it was referred and before the award was pronounced by the 
Tribunal, so would it be open to the parties to settle the dispute so long 
as it was pending either before the Labour Appellate Tribunal or be 
fore this Court. " 


From the above observation , it is clear that even during the pendency of a case 
betore a Tribunal the parties can legitimately compromise the dispute. In the 
last but one paragraph of the judgment at page 711, the following observation clinchen 
the whole matter : - 


" The compromise in question is intended to be Aled in this Court for the 

purpose of enabling the parties to request this Court to pass an order 
in terms of the said compromise. The procedure for obtaining such 
and order which was to be followed is the procedure prescribed by the 
rules of this Court, just as, if a compromise was reached before the 
Tribunal, the procedure to be followed before it would be the procedure 
prescribed by its rules . " 


From the above observation , it is evident that when a compromise is Aled before 
a tribunal. the tribunal has to ascertain firstly , the fact of the compromise and 
secondly the validity thereof. Even though , therefore , provisions of Order XXIII 
Rule 3 C . P . C . may not be available to the tribunal, yet on general principles it a 
valid compromise has been reached it would be the duty of the tribunal to examine 
the same and accept the same ir found valid . After all, a compromise is a contract 
and under Section 10 of the Indian Contract Act all agreements are contracts if 
they are made hy the free consent of the parties competent to contract, for a lawful 
consideration and with a lawful object and are not hereby expressly declared to be 
void Consequently , the rule incorporated in Order XXIII R . 3 C . P . C . 18 baged on 
the sound principle of freedom of contracts and the requirement are the same as are 
found in the definition of Section 10 of the Contract Act Even though , therefore , 
C . XXIII R . 3 CPC may not be applicable yet the tribunal would apply the general 
principles, particularly when the object of Industrial adjudication is to achieve in 
dustrial peace and settle the disputes on considerations of social justice . When no 
rules of procedure ale prescribed regarding any compromise arrived at during the 
pendency of a case before the Trlbunal, the general princinle will apply on the line 
indicated by the Hon ble Supreme Court in Swadeshi Cotton Mills case ( supra ) 


6 . It is , therefore, held that the Corporation has not got an unfettered right to 
resile from the compromise arrived at in these cases and the fact of the compromise 
together with its validity will have to be enquired into as a question of fact. The 
parties will, therefore , be directed to adduce necessary evidence on the point and 
will be informed accordingly . 


7 . After the order was dictated on 20th September 1967 and before it could be 
transcribed and signed the representative of the management, Shri K , C . Goel, has 
applied that the application dated 30th August 1987 reslling from the compromise 
is not being pressed and the management would abide by the compromige gettle 
ment arrived at on 4th August 1967. That being so , there is no necessity left to call 
for evidence on the point of fact whether there was a compromise entered into and 
the same was valld and lastul one.

INDUSTRY . Copper 

DISTRUCT : Singhbhum 


ORDER / AWARD 
The Complainant, Sri S . B . Barua , Aled an application before the Central 
Industrial Tribunal, Dhanbad , under Section 33 - A I. D . Act complaining that 
during the pendency of an industrial dispute covered by Ref. No. 34 / 65 of Dhan 
bad Tribunal the opposite party , the Indian Copper Corporation Ltd , changed 
the conditions of his Service Inasmuch as he was denied Acting Allowance by 
means of an order dated 15th March , 1965 . The parent reference along with 
this case was tranferred to this Tribunal by Notification No. 8 / 25 / 67 - LRII ” dated 
26th April, 1967. 

The opposite party in the first instance raised a question whether the comp 
lainant was a concerned workman in the parent reference and after thig ques 
tion was decided in favour of the complainant, date for hearing was fixed on 
other issues in the case which were framed on 22nd June 1987 at Camp Allahabad 
Before , however , evidence could be recorded at camp Mosaboni, parties had 
settled their dispute and Aled a compromise petition which is Annexure " A 
to this order . The management, however, tried to resile from the compromise 
settlement and on their so doing arguments were heard on the legal aspect of 
the matter and a comprehensive order which is Annexure " B " to this order was 
passed on 20 / 28 September , 1967. The management later on by means of an 
application dated 28th September 1967 withdrew their application and adhered to 
the compromise settlement which is in the nature of a pack deal setting a 
number of their cases including this case , Para 2 of the compromise settlement 
would show that the management has agreed to pay the Acting Allowance for 
18 days to the complainant and this resolver the controversy in favour of 
the complainant. The controversy is thus satisfactorily resolved in favour of 
the complainant and an order is recorded accordingly . 

Since the order announts to an award , let the same be sent to the Govern 
ment of India , Ministry of Labour and Employment, for publication in the 
Gazette . 


$ d / - G C AGARWALA , 

Presiding Officer ,

Short Recital of the case : 

Whereas in its letters No. MMLU / 103 /MS /64 dated 3rd October 1964 and No 
MMLU / G - 3A ( CO ) /64 - 3 dated 8th November 1964 the Mosaboni Mines Labour 
Union raised Industrial Disputes for reinstatement of Shri Bhupati Sen (Badge 
No . 5998 ) and for supply of free electricity and fuel to Shri U . Patnaik (Badge 
Vo. 1706 ) respectively . 

And whereas these disputes were fererred by the Government of India in 
the Ministry of Labour, Employment and Rehabilitation to the Central Govern 
ment Industrial Tribunal for adjudication . 

And whereas during the pendency of the aforesaid references before the Indus 
trial Tribunal, various application u / s 33 ( 2 ) ( b ) and u / s 33 ( 3 ) ( b ) of the Industrial 
Disputes Act have been made by the Management and complaints u / s 33A of the 
Industrial Disputes Act have been made by the certain workmen . 

And whercas the aforesaid two References are pending as No. 72 of 1967 and 
No. 82 of 1967 before the Industrial Tribunal -calm -Labour Court (Central ) at 
Jabalpur and the cases under Sections 33 and 33A I. D . Act are also pending before 
the same Tribunal and numbered as detailed in the Schedule A and B annexed 
hereto . 

And whereas the Parties hereto , with a view to create better understanding , 
promote healthy industrial Relations and maintain Industrial peace , after repeat 
ed discussions have arrived at an amicable settlement on this Friday, the 4th of 
August 1967 in the following pets anti con litions :

decided to commence hearing and in fact main Reference No. 82 /67 and another 
case under Section 33 - A No. 22 /67 were heard and evidence was recorded in 
subsequent dates . When the hearing commenced on 31st August, 1967 at camp 
Mosaboni applications were filed on behalf of the management in 19 cases that 
the management would not abide by the settlement for certain reasons stated 
in the applications, the settlement having been brought about under false re 
presentation and was voidable at the option of the Corporation Compromise 
petition is annexure " C " to this order . The application of the management in 
Annexure " D " . Aller hearing preliininary arguments on this application of tho 
management it was decided to hear ful-fledged arguments purely on the point of 
law whether the management has an unfettered right to resile from the com 
promise seiflement and oth September , 1967 was fixed for the purpose at camp 
Dhanbad . It was cxpressly staled that purely the legal aspect of the question 
should be detailed and not on thc justifiability or otherwise of the action of the 
management. The representatives of the parties argued the law point but with 
aut rendering any real assistance to the Tribunal on the question of law . Some 
cases were cited by the representative of the management, most of which were 
irrelevant. None was cited on behalf of the Union . I have , however, endeavour 
ad to find out any parallel case for guidance but I must confess that I could 
not come across any in my search . It is presumably because this is a unique 
caso of its kind wherein the parties having arrived at a pack deal for compromige 
of cases pending before the Tribunal, one of them , rightly or wrongly felt aggriev 
od and attempted to resile from the position before the compromise could be 
dramined and accepted in all the cases so as to be incorporated in the awards 
of this Tribunal and before a finality could be achieved . With the above narra 
tion of facts and observations, the legal position may now be examined . 


2 . The Industrial Disputes Act defines a gettlement in Section 2 ( p ) as follows: - - 


" settlement" means a settlement arrived at in the course of conciliation 

proceedings and includes a written agreement between the employer 
and workmen arrived at otherwise than in the course of conciliation 
proceeding where such agreement has been signed by the parties 
thereto in such manner as may be prescribed and a copy thereof has 
been sent to the appropriate Government and the Concillation omcer " . 


It defines a settlement in two categories , namely , those which are arrived at in 
the course of conciliation proceedings and such which are arrived at otherwise than 
In the course of conciliation proceedings. For the second category , there are certain 
requirements which have been prescribed under Rule 58 of Industrial Disputes ( Cen 
tral ) Rules , hesides the requirements that a copy of the same has to be sent to 
appropriate Government and the Conciliation Officer . Such a settlement arrived 
at otherwise than in the course of conciliation proceedings under Section 18 ( 1 ) It 
1s binding on the parties to the agreement only . The first category of settlement 
arrived at in the course of conciliation proceedings as also arbitration award or 
an award of Labour Court of Tribunal under Sub - section ( 3 ) of Section 18 they 
are binding on all parties to the industrial dispute including the entire body of 
workmen as stated in Clause 3 ( d ) . The Art makes no provision about the position 
of a settlement arīlyed at otherwise than in conciliation proceedings but during 
the pendency of an industrial dispute before a Labour Court or Tribunal. When 
a reference is pending before a Labour Court or Tribunal whether a compromise 
petition fulAls the requirements of the definition of Section 2 ( p ) I. D . Act or not , If 
the same is a reasonable and just settlement and is accepted by the Labour Court or 
Tribunal, it is incorporated in the award and then Section 18 ( 3 ) is attracted . This 
power of the Labour Court or Tribunal to accept the compromise in the award 
is no more in doubt. In Poona Mazdoor Sabha V . Dhutta and another reported 
in 1956 - II -LLJ. p . 319 at page 321 Chagla C . J. made some doubtful observation as 
tollows: 


" Industrial law takes no notice of any private settlement or agreement arriv 

ed at between the parties in the course of an Industrial dispute . Such 
a private agreement belongs to the realın of contract ; it may give 
rise to contractual rights ; but when we are dealing with industrial law 
it has no sanction whatsoever. " 


but whethe real an inaut,por 


284 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : JANUARY 20 , 1988 / PAUSA 30 , 1889 


(PART 11 


But another portion of the judgment at page 323 however, nullified the sting of the 
above observation . It runs as follows: 
" Industrial peace demands that sanctity should be attached to agreements 

freely arrived at by the parties and if the view went abroad that prl 
vate settlements have no sanctity whatsover, then there would be little 
chance of disputes ending by settlement between the parties . It will in 
deed by extremely unfortunate from the point of view of labour." 


From the aforesaid subsequent observation of the Learned Chief Justice , it is clear 
that sanctity was attached to agreements freely arrived at and should be honoured 
and acted upon by the Tribunals . The Kerala High Court in 1957 ( II) LL . J. p . 45 
Krishnakutty Nair V . Industrial Tribunal considered this question as also the 
rights of the parties to adjust their differences wholly or in part by lawful agree 
ments or compromise. The following is a material passage which needs reproduc 
tion : 
" Now , it is the elementary right of the parties to a dispute to adjust their 

matters of difference wholly or in part , by any lawful agreement or 
compromise. And every authority entrusted with the task of adjudl 
cating , the dispute was a duty , not a discretion , to record such agree 
ment or compromise placed before it by the disputants . There is nothing 
when substantial injustice would be worked . A Tribunal therefore, to 
whom an industrial dispute is referred for adjudication under Section 
10 ( 1 ) ( C ) of the Industrial Disputes Act , must record a lawful agree 
ment or compromise placed before it by the disputants . There is nothing 
in the Act to suggest that a particular class of agreement or compro 
mise is outside its scope, though whether a particular compromise lo 
lawful or not will vary with the varying character of different disputes . 
The expression " determination " in the definitiou of " Award " in the 
Act indicates only a coming to an end , may be in any way whatever , 
though it may require examination and choice " 


On the principle enunciated in the above ruling, the Industrial Tribunals have 
been accepting compromise agrcements arrived at frecly and lawfully and incorporat 
ed the same in their awards. The question , however, is whether a party having 
entered into a lawful agrecment has an unfettered and inherent right without show 
ing just cause to resile from the same. There is no direct case of law on the point 
but a few w ch ve some relevancy may be stated . 

3 . The Madras High Court in workers cmployed in 32 textiles mills , Coimbatore 
District Mill Workers Union Vs. Dhanalakshmi Mills Lid 1960 (II) LL . J p . 556 — 
A . I. R . 1900 (Mad ) page 212 observed thai the Industrial Tribunals have no power 
similar to one under Order XXII . R . 3 C . P . C . to record a compromise . In the 
next paragraph , however , the position was made clear by the following passage : 
" this does not, however , mean that the Tribunal iş precluded from taking note 

of a compromise entered into between the workers and the management. 
Where there is a compromise , it should consider whether, in its opinion , 
the compromise could be adopted as its own determination of the 
dispute , i. e , whether it is fall , just and equitable between the parties ." 


4 . In the ruling under consideration , three of the Unions and the management 
of the mills entered into a compromise agreement Another Union which was 
not a party to the agrecment but represented a substantial number of workmen 
of the mills did not accept the compromise and desired determination by the Tri 
hunal. The Tribunal overruled the objection and accepting the compromise agree 
ment recorded an award . It was in this context that the Hon ble High Court mado 
the above observation and held that as the Union was cntitled to be heard under 
Section 36 I. D . Act , the Tribunal was wrong in ignoring the request and accepting 
the compromise . 

5 . The Allahabad High Court in a case Raza Textile Labour Union Vs. R . Mohan 
reported in I.FLR. 1964 (8 ) p 306 which has some bearing on the question consi 
dered the effect of a settlement before a statutory arbitrator under U . P . Industrial 
Disputes Act and which was not registered as required by Section 6B of the U . P . 
Act. This Section requires an agreement to be registered before the Concillation 
Omcer in the prescribed manner if arrived at outside conciliation proceedings . It 
was hold that Section 6 B was a self contained provision and the unregistered settle 
ment incorporated in the award was binding by virtue of Section 6 ( 5 ) of the Act. 

The case therefore, is of no material hcl except for the position that a settle 
ment not having been brought about in a prescribed manner can nevertheless be 


Suc , B (Tl)] 


THE C 


ETTE U . Lx LA : JANE 


2 


acted upon . The material clue , however , is furnished by the observation of the 
Hon ble Supreme Court in Swadeshi Cotton Mills Vs. Rajeswar Prasad and others 
reported in 1960 - II - LL . J . p . 707 . That was a case where after the decision of the 
Labour Appellate Tribunal, the parties entered into a compromise in respect of an 
appeal pending before the Hon ble Court . The Supreme Court framed two issues , 
one on the factual aspect of the compromise and second , whether it was a valid 
one, and remitted them for a finding from the Tribunal. The Tribunal found that 
the compromise had in fact taken place and was a valid one . The Supreme Court 
accepted the findings and disposed of the appeal in terms of the compromise . At 
page 710 , the following material observation was made : 
" Just as an industrial dispute could have been settled between the parties 

either before it was referred for adjudication to the Industrial Tribunal, 
or after it was referred and before the award was pronounced by the 
Tribunal, so would it be open to the parties to settle the dispute so 
long as it was pending either before the Labour Appellate Tribunal or 

before this Court ." 
From the above obseryation , it is clear that even during the pendency of a case be 
forec Tribunal the parties can legitimately compromise in dispute. In the last 
but paragraph of the judgment at page 711, the following observation clinches 
the vole matter : 
" The compromise in question is intended to be flled in this Court for the 

purpose of enabling the parties to request this Court to pass an order 
in terms of the said compromise. The procedure for obtaining such 
an order which was to be followed is the procedure prescribed by the 
rules of this Court, just as, if a compromise was reached before the 
Tribunal, the procedure to be followed before it would be the proce 
dure prescribed by its rules ." 


From the above observation , it is evident that when a compromise is Aled before 
a tribunal, the tribunal has to ascertain firstly , the fact of the compromise and se 
condly the validity thereof. Even though , therefore, provisions of Order XXIII 
Rule 3 C . P . C . may not be available to the tribunal , yet on general principles if a 
valld compromise has been reached it would be the duty of the tribunal to examine 
the same and accept the same if found valid After all, a compromise is a contract 
and under Section 10 of the Indian Contract Act " all agreements are contracts it 
They are made by the free consent of the parties competent to contract, for a 
lawful consideration and with a lawful object and arc not hereby expressly declared 
to be vold " . Consequently , the rule incorporated in Order XXIII R . 3 C . P . C . 16 
based on the sound principle of freedom of contracts and the requirements are the 
same ns are found in the definition of Section 10 of the Contract Act. Even though , 
therefore , C . XXIII R . 3 C . P . C . may not be applicable yet the tribunal would apply 
the general principles , particularly when the object of industrial adjudication is 
to achieve industrial peace and settle the disputes on considerations of social justice . 
When no rules of procedure are prescribed regarding any compromise arrived at 
during the pendency of a case before the Tribunal, the general principle will apply 
on the lines Indicated by the Hon ble Supreme Court in Swadeshi Cotton Mills case 
( supra ) , 


6 . It is , therefore, hold that the Corporation has not got an unfettered right to 
resile from the compromise arrived at in these cases and the fact of the compro 
mise to gather with its validity will have to be enquired into as a question of fact. 
The parties will, therefore , be directed to adduce necessary evidence on the point 
and will be informed accordingly . 


7. After the order was dictated on 20th September, 1967 and before it could be 
transcribed and signed the representative of the management, Shri K . C . Goel, has 
applied that the application doled 30th August , 1987 restling from the compromise 
is not being pressed and the management would abide by the compromise settlement 
arrived at on 4th August , 1967. That being so , there is no necessity left to call for 
evidence on the point of fact whether therc was a compromise entered into and the 
game was valid and lawful one .

the Chief Inspector of Mines to exempt, for the period upto the 30th June, 1988, 
Any mine from the operation of clauses ( 3 ) and ( 37 ) of regulation 2 of the Meta 
ulterous Mines Regulations, 1961, in so far as such clauses provide that an Assis 
tant Manager or Underground Manager , as the case may be, should possess : 
Mapager s Certificate if the Chief Inspector is of opinion that the conditions in 
Buch mines are such as to render compliance with such provision unnecessary 
or impraeticable ,
